## [MiniMax H3 Day-0 Support in ComfyUI: Open Weights, Native Audio, 2K Video](https://blog.comfy.org/p/minimax-h3-day-0-support-in-comfyui) ⭐️ 8.0/10

ComfyUI announced day-0 support for MiniMax H3, an open-weights omni-modal model that generates up to 15-second 2K videos with native stereo audio from text, images, video, and audio inputs. The model's memory footprint is reduced by 66% through pruning, enabling local execution on consumer GPUs like the RTX 3060. This marks a significant step for open-weights video generation, as MiniMax H3 combines multiple modalities and high-resolution output with practical local deployment. Day-0 support in ComfyUI lowers the barrier for creators and developers to experiment with state-of-the-art video models, potentially accelerating innovation in AI-generated content. The pruning technique replaces modulation weights (about 40% of parameters) with a functionally equivalent lookup table, reducing memory from 123.6 GB to 42.5 GB with no loss in output quality. Combined with dynamic VRAM offloading, the model can run on a 16GB RTX 3060, though generation times may be long (e.g., 10 minutes for a 10-second 480p clip on a 4070 Ti Super).

**Background**: MiniMax H3 is a general-purpose omni-modal generation model that can understand and generate across text, images, video, and audio. ComfyUI is a popular node-based interface for AI image and video generation, and 'day-0 support' means the model is natively integrated on its release day. Model pruning is a technique to reduce model size by removing redundant parameters while maintaining accuracy, which is crucial for deploying large models on consumer hardware.

Bonsai demonstrates OCaml's viability in frontend development, allowing developers to use the same language and types on both backend and frontend, potentially reducing bugs and improving code reuse. <a id="item-6"></a>
## [Rust Project Goals: Immobile Types and Guaranteed Destructors](https://github.com/rust-lang/rust-project-goals/blob/main/src/2026/move-trait.md) ⭐️ 8.0/10

The Rust project has officially accepted a project goal to explore adding immobile types and guaranteed destructors to the language, potentially replacing the current Pin hack. This goal, outlined in the 2026 roadmap, marks a significant step toward addressing long-standing limitations in Rust's type system. This development could fundamentally improve Rust's support for self-referential types, which are crucial for async futures and other patterns, while also enabling safe scoped spawn and other patterns that require guaranteed cleanup. It addresses a glaring hole in the language that has existed since 2016, potentially reshaping how systems programming is done in Rust. The proposal introduces the concept of immobile types, which cannot be moved after creation, and guaranteed destructors, which ensure cleanup code runs reliably. It also mentions 'must-move types' (linear types) as a related but separate feature, and the design may change significantly or be abandoned entirely, as it is not yet an accepted language change.

hackernews · paavohtl · Aug 3, 06:42 · [Discussion](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49152023)

**Background**: Rust has historically assumed that all values can be moved and forgotten (via mem::forget), which prevents safe self-referential types. The Pin type was introduced as a workaround, but it is considered a hack. Immobile types would allow self-referential futures and other patterns without requiring unsafe code, while guaranteed destructors would ensure cleanup runs even in the presence of mem::forget.

<details><summary>References</summary>
<ul>
<li><a href="https://github.com/rust-lang/rust-project-goals/blob/main/src/2026/move-trait.md">rust-project-goals/src/2026/move-trait.md at main · rust-lang/rust-project-goals</a></li>
<li><a href="https://rust-lang.github.io/rust-project-goals/2026/move-trait.html">Immobile types and guaranteed destructors - Rust Project Goals</a></li>
<li><a href="https://rust-lang.github.io/rfcs/2349-pin.html">2349-pin - The Rust RFC Book</a></li>

</ul>
</details>

**Discussion**: Community comments express enthusiasm for the proposal, noting it fills a long-standing gap, but also caution that it is not yet an accepted change and the design may evolve. There is discussion about alternative approaches, such as 'pinned places', and clarification that linear types are a separate but related concept. Some commenters highlight the complexity of guaranteed destructors, comparing it to C++ features.

**Tags**: `#Rust`, `#language design`, `#immovable types`, `#destructors`, `#linear types`

<a id="item-8"></a>
## [AI Scientist Benchmarking: FARS Outperforms in Automated Review](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28631) ⭐️ 8.0/10

This paper introduces a benchmarking protocol using automated multi-model peer review to evaluate AI Scientist systems. It finds that FARS benchmark papers significantly outperform Sakana AI (v1 & v2), CycleResearcher, and Data-to-Paper, achieving mean scores of 2.14–2.47 on a 1–5 scale compared to 1.00–1.87 for other systems. This establishes the first quantitative benchmark for AI Scientist systems, addressing the critical challenge of evaluating AI-generated research. It demonstrates that multi-model LLM evaluation provides a scalable and consistent framework, which could influence future research evaluation methods and guide the development of autonomous research systems. The study used three independent LLM reviewers (GPT-5.4, Gemini, and Claude) to evaluate 60 papers generated by four frameworks plus 15 FARS benchmark papers. Gemini and Claude showed strong agreement (ρ = 0.907, p < 0.001), while GPT-5.4 exhibited weaker agreement (ρ ≈ 0.32), suggesting different evaluation criteria.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 3, 04:00

**Background**: AI Scientist systems are autonomous frameworks that can generate research papers with minimal human intervention, aiming to accelerate scientific discovery. Evaluating the quality of these AI-generated papers is challenging because traditional peer review is time-consuming and subjective. This paper proposes using frontier large language models as automated reviewers to provide a scalable and consistent evaluation across multiple dimensions, including originality, rigor, clarity, and significance.

<a id="item-9"></a>
## [ThinkReset: Learnable Interface for Long-Horizon Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28642) ⭐️ 8.0/10

The paper introduces ThinkReset, a novel method that constructs reusable intermediate interfaces via interface writeback and reset, directly optimizing post-reset continuation success for long-horizon reasoning under bounded context windows. This addresses critical issues like context overflow and error anchoring in long chain-of-thought reasoning, potentially improving AI performance on complex multi-step problems without requiring larger context windows. It offers a new perspective that could influence future LLM reasoning architectures and training strategies. ThinkReset operates in text space, constructing reusable intermediate interfaces through writeback and reset, and optimizes post-reset continuation success. The method consistently improves success rates across multiple long-horizon reasoning benchmarks under fixed context windows, as reported in the preprint.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 3, 04:00

**Background**: Long chain-of-thought reasoning can improve performance but suffers from redundancy accumulation, context overflow, and error anchoring. Under bounded context windows, the core bottleneck is the absence of a reusable intermediate interface that can replace discarded history. ThinkReset addresses this by constructing such interfaces, allowing the model to continue solving after a reset.

<a id="item-10"></a>
## [SARE: A Geometric Framework to Measure Step-Wise Reasoning Effort in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28674) ⭐️ 8.0/10

The paper introduces Step-Aware Reasoning Energy (SARE), a novel geometric framework that quantifies computational effort at individual chain-of-thought (CoT) steps using Centered Kernel Alignment (CKA) between Gram matrices of token hidden states across adjacent transformer layers. It reveals non-uniform energy allocation across reasoning steps, with incorrect trajectories showing systematically lower energy at critical junctions. This work addresses a significant gap in LLM interpretability by providing step-wise granularity for reasoning effort, which existing methods lack. It could enable better understanding and improvement of LLM reasoning, with potential applications in confidence estimation and error detection. SARE captures inter-token relational structure without requiring eigenvector alignment or cluster correspondence, and it models CoT trajectories as transitions among latent semantic states. The framework was validated across six reasoning benchmarks and three open-weight LLMs, showing that SARE-based features match or outperform output-based confidence baselines in most settings.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 3, 04:00

Centered Kernel Alignment (CKA) is a statistical tool that measures similarity between neural network representations by comparing centered Gram matrices, providing a way to compare hidden states across layers.

## [LLMs Not Yet Safe for Autonomous Clinical Triage](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28677) ⭐️ 8.0/10

A new perspective paper argues that large language models (LLMs) are not yet safe for autonomous clinical decision support, particularly for triaging undifferentiated patients without clinician oversight. The authors highlight a fundamental mismatch between text prediction and safe sequential decision-making under asymmetric costs. This paper addresses a critical safety issue in the rapidly growing use of LLMs in healthcare, where autonomous systems could make high-stakes decisions. It underscores the need for rigorous evaluation frameworks that incorporate incomplete information and asymmetric cost stress testing before clinical deployment. The paper proposes that claims about autonomous clinical decision support should be validated with incomplete information and asymmetric cost stress testing on synthetic and real patient encounters. It identifies failure modes such as credulity, agreeableness, and miscalibration, which can be amplified by assistant-like behaviors.

However, safe triage requires sequential decision-making under uncertainty, where the cost of missing a critical diagnosis is far higher than false alarms. Current evaluations often use complete, well-curated datasets, which may not reflect real-world conditions where patients may not volunteer key information.

## [Validity Audit of Agent-Safety Benchmarks Reveals Scoring Flaws](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28685) ⭐️ 8.0/10

A new paper audits four agent-safety benchmarks (R-Judge, InjecAgent, AgentHarm, AgentDojo) and finds that an 'always positive' policy can achieve an F1 score of 0.690 on R-Judge, outperforming five of 21 models that actually discriminate. The study also shows that benchmark rankings are unstable due to small-panel artifacts, with correlations shifting from -0.64 to +0.02 depending on the model subset. This audit challenges the reliability of widely used agent-safety benchmarks, which are often quoted interchangeably as measures of an agent's safety. The findings imply that current safety evaluations may be misleading, potentially affecting AI safety research, model development, and policy decisions. The paper evaluates up to 22 models under official implementations and author-provided scorers, with MMLU and GPQA as capability composites. It finds that capability predicts task success (ρ=+0.60) but correlates negatively with misalignment safety (ρ=-0.44, n=21), and the contrast on a paired n=20 panel is Δ=-1.00 (95% CI [-1.48, -0.49], p<0.001). On an expanded 41-model panel, the misalignment correlation weakens to -0.16, and jailbreak strengthens to +0.34, though neither change is significant.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 3, 04:00

**Background**: Agent-safety benchmarks are designed to evaluate the safety of AI agents, which are systems that can interact with environments and tools. Common benchmarks include R-Judge, which tests safety risk awareness, InjecAgent for indirect prompt injection attacks, AgentHarm for harmful multi-step tasks, and AgentDojo for security. These benchmarks are often used to compare models, but their validity as safety measures has not been thoroughly examined.

The agent uses an evolving memory of skills, experiences, and an ontologized tool graph, distilling generalizable knowledge from contrastive trajectories. During inference, it employs a LinUCB-based bandit gate to balance exploration and exploitation, and completes the ontology of newly acquired tools online for seamless integration.

Researchers introduced EarlyDx, a large-scale benchmark built from 154,834 emergency department encounters in MIMIC-IV, designed for open-ended early diagnosis using only admission-time data and LLM-based verification of evidence-supported labels. EarlyDx addresses limitations of existing diagnosis-prediction benchmarks by enabling open-ended generation, incorporating free-text notes, and supervising with ED diagnoses rather than discharge diagnoses. This could significantly advance AI-assisted clinical decision support research and improve early diagnosis accuracy in emergency settings. The benchmark uses an LLM auditor to verify each free-text label as supported, partially supported, or unsupported, with primary evaluation scoring only fully supported labels. Under a semantic LLM-as-judge protocol, no evaluated system—including frontier general, medical-specialized, or in-domain post-trained—reliably synthesizes admission-time evidence, with zero-shot models recovering only 3-31% of inferred diagnoses and post-training raising inference-dependent recall to 56%.

This paper introduces an interaction-centric taxonomy that localizes agent failures to specific component interactions, organizing 41 failure modes by assigning each to an edge between two components and a fault side. The taxonomy is grounded in examples from public benchmarks and evaluated with independent reasoning agents, achieving Cohen's kappa of 0.76 against human labels. This taxonomy addresses the repair-assignment problem in AI agent systems, enabling developers to identify whether a failure requires model post-training, harness engineering, or environment redesign. It provides a shared structure across different agent architectures, potentially improving debugging efficiency and accelerating agent development. The taxonomy assigns each failure mode to an interaction edge and a fault side (model-side or harness-side), making it actionable for repair. It was evaluated using four frontier models as judges, with the strongest achieving Cohen's kappa of 0.76, indicating good reproducibility.

The paper presents a formal model showing conditions under which an AI with an imperfect proxy for human values can become catastrophic under optimization, highlighting the dangers of overoptimization. This work addresses a core concern in AI safety by providing formal results on the fragility of value under imperfect alignment, which is highly relevant to the field. It motivates AI designs that limit optimization pressure, such as quantilizers, rather than relying solely on pre-deployment training. The model assumes an agent undergoes idealized alignment training that guarantees its value function satisfies a proxy condition before optimizing the world. The primary results identify conditions on the human value function and the accuracy of several proxy conditions under which an agent with an η-catastrophic value function would be deployed.

SciDisco introduces a scalable framework for training scientific discovery agents using process-verifiable environments and turn-level reinforcement learning, achieving state-of-the-art performance with the SciDisco-14B model on hypothesis-driven scientific data analysis benchmarks. This addresses the critical gap of process supervision in long-horizon scientific analysis, enabling more reliable and efficient AI-driven discovery. It could accelerate research in fields like biology, chemistry, and materials science by automating complex data analysis tasks. The framework comprises three components: SciThèque compiles hypotheses, datasets, hidden evidence graphs, and verifiers into task environments; DAG-grounded trajectory synthesis generates verifier-filtered multi-turn demonstrations; and DiscoPO assigns turn-level credit to actions producing verifiable analytical evidence. Experiments show SciDisco-14B achieves state-of-the-art results.

## [CAGE: Joint Certification for Tool-Using LLM Agents Under Return Uncertainty](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29190) ⭐️ 8.0/10

CAGE introduces a method to certify authorization for tool-using LLM agents under uncertainty in tool returns, proving that separate categorical and numerical certification is insufficient and offering a joint certification approach that directly certifies the joint neighborhood. This work addresses a critical gap in AI safety for tool-using agents, as runtime permission gates typically authorize observed returns without accounting for small errors in binding. By providing a formal certification method, CAGE enhances the reliability and safety of LLM agents in real-world applications, potentially enabling more autonomous decisions while maintaining safety guarantees. CAGE enumerates discrete branches exactly and certifies continuous perturbations within each branch. It offers three variants: CAGE-Exact for executable policies, and CAGE-Lip and CAGE-RS for learned gates under explicit fidelity assumptions, tested across synthetic, policy-as-code, regulatory, and real-transaction settings.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 3, 04:00

**Background**: Tool-using LLM agents act on typed tool returns, which pair provenance and categorical fields with numerical values. Runtime permission gates authorize observed returns and actions, but they are vulnerable to small errors in how returns are bound to their sources. CAGE addresses this by certifying authorization over a declared neighborhood of plausible correctly bound returns, considering one admissible binding fault and bounded numerical drift.

## [Tool Specs Weaken LLM Agent Safety; SafeKeep Mitigates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29254) ⭐️ 8.0/10

A new arXiv paper identifies schema-formatted tool specifications as a primary cause of safety degradation in LLM-based AI agents, and introduces SafeKeep, an inference-time safeguard that improves refusal rates and reduces attack success without harming task performance. This finding reveals a previously overlooked safety risk in AI agents, which are increasingly used for real-world actions. SafeKeep offers a practical, model-agnostic mitigation that can be applied to existing systems, potentially improving safety across the LLM agent ecosystem. SafeKeep decouples safety judgment from tool execution by using flattened textual tool specifications for safety assessment while retaining schema-formatted specs for execution. Across two benchmarks and four LLMs (white-box and black-box), it raised average refusal rates from 23.8% to 70.6% and cut attack success under prompt injection from 25.6% to 2.5%.

**Background**: AI agents extend LLMs with external tools, enabling complex tasks but also introducing safety risks. Tool specifications are often formatted as JSON schemas, which are efficient for execution but may weaken the model's internal refusal signals, leading to unsafe actions. SafeKeep addresses this by using a different format for safety checks.

A new survey synthesizes 257 papers to characterize validation challenges for agentic AI systems, proposing a five-dimension taxonomy covering behavioral, safety, temporal, regulatory, and multi-agent concerns. It reveals that temporal validity, runtime evidence maintenance, regulatory legibility, and open-ended multi-agent assurance remain under-developed. As agentic AI systems become more prevalent in safety-critical domains like healthcare and industrial operations, this survey provides a crucial framework for understanding and addressing validation gaps. It highlights the need for validating trajectories in context rather than isolated components, which is essential for trustworthy deployment. The survey organizes current approaches using a five-dimension taxonomy and identifies that behavioral evaluation is comparatively mature, while temporal validity, runtime monitoring, and regulatory compliance are under-addressed. It includes three cross-domain case studies (medical care, industrial operations, smart-mobility) and proposes a lifecycle-oriented research agenda with bounded-autonomy specifications and adversarial trajectory generation.

## [ModelEquivBench: Certifying Multi-Relational Evaluation of LLM Optimization Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29431) ⭐️ 8.0/10

ModelEquivBench introduces a certifying, multi-relational evaluation system that reports a per-pair semantic profile E0–E6 for LLM-generated optimization models, providing independently checkable evidence for each decided relation. It was used to evaluate three model snapshots (GPT-5.4, Claude Sonnet 4.6, Qwen3.5-397B-A17B) on 173 base problems, revealing distinctions that coarse baselines miss. This work addresses a critical gap in evaluating LLM-generated optimization models, moving beyond binary equivalence or execution success rates to a fine-grained, certifiable semantic profile. It provides a more faithful and trustworthy evaluation method, likely influencing future research and tooling in LLM-based optimization modeling. The E0–E6 profile includes model construction and exact ingestion (E0), verified representation alignment (E1), same-space and projected feasible-set relations (E2, E3), objective-order equivalence (E4), optimal-value equality (E5), and optimizer-set equivalence (E6). Each decided entry carries replayable traces, exact-rational certificates, or explicit witnesses, while incomplete mapping search or unsupported structure yields typed UNKNOWN or N/A outcomes.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 3, 04:00

**Background**: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used to generate optimization models from natural language descriptions. Traditional evaluation methods often reduce the comparison between a generated model and its ground truth to a single equivalence verdict or an execution success rate, which are neither independently checkable nor capture the multiple ways two formulations can agree. ModelEquivBench addresses this by providing a multi-relational certification framework with formal evidence.

This benchmark addresses a gap in current AI evaluation suites by focusing on rules-rich tactical reasoning, which is under-tested in existing benchmarks. It could drive progress in AI for complex decision-making and reinforcement learning, benefiting both game AI and general AI research. DungeonBench provides a complete tactical observation, a pending decision, and an indexed list of executable options at each step, supporting heuristic controllers, language-model policies, learned option rankers, and masked-action RL agents. Evaluations show that frontier language-model policies often win direct encounters but fail in linked days due to poor resource budgeting and rest timing.

## [AgentHPOBench: New Benchmark for LLM Agents in Hyperparameter Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29626) ⭐️ 8.0/10

AgentHPOBench is a new benchmark comprising 30 executable machine learning tasks across seven research categories, designed to evaluate LLM agents' ability to perform sequential hyperparameter optimization based on experimental evidence. The paper evaluates 12 widely used agents and conventional HPO baselines under a unified protocol. This benchmark fills a gap in existing evaluations that focus on static code generation or final answer correctness, by directly assessing whether agents can interpret experimental evidence and guide subsequent hyperparameter decisions. It provides valuable insights for the AI/ML community, highlighting current limitations and guiding future improvements in autonomous scientific agents. Each task in AgentHPOBench begins with a validated baseline run, after which an agent performs several sequential interventions, observing accumulated configurations, metrics, and logs before proposing the next valid configuration. The results show that current agents exhibit measurable experimental optimization ability but face limitations in sustained iterative refinement, complex log diagnosis, and consistent progress toward reference performance.

**Background**: LLM agents are evolving from code completion systems into autonomous scientific agents capable of conducting experiments. ## [Topology-Aware Orchestrator Cuts KV Cache Transfer Latency in Disaggregated GPU Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28633) ⭐️ 8.0/10

Researchers propose a topology-aware data movement orchestrator for disaggregated LLM inference that discovers interconnect hierarchy at startup and selects optimal transport per transfer, hiding 60-85% of KV cache transfer latency behind computation. It also introduces NVLink domain-aware placement for MoE models and CXL 3.0 memory expanders as a shared overflow tier. This addresses a critical unsolved problem in disaggregated LLM inference, where KV cache transfer between prefill and decode pools becomes a major bottleneck at production scale. By exploiting interconnect hierarchy, the approach could significantly improve inference latency and throughput, impacting the design of future AI infrastructure. The paper reports that bandwidth between GPUs varies by 72x depending on physical relationship: 900 GB/s via NVLink within a domain, 50 GB/s via InfiniBand across nodes, and 12.5 GB/s via TCP across data centers. The proposed mechanisms include pipelined layer-by-layer transfer, NVLink domain-aware placement for MoE, and CXL 3.0 memory expanders providing 6x capacity at 86x lower latency than NVMe. Full evaluation requires hardware not yet available in academic or cloud environments.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 3, 04:00

**Background**: Disaggregated LLM inference splits prefill and decode phases onto separate GPU pools, requiring the KV cache to be transferred between them. Existing systems like DistServe, Splitwise, and Mooncake use uniform RDMA, ignoring the interconnect hierarchy. KV caching stores intermediate attention keys and values to avoid recomputation, but transferring large KV caches (e.g., 2.6 GB per request for a 70B model) creates a networking bottleneck.

## [Knowledge Distillation Has Asymmetric Effects on Bias in Small Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28639) ⭐️ 8.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2607.28639) shows that knowledge distillation in small instruction-tuned language models has asymmetric effects on bias: it improves context-following on unambiguous tasks (BBQ-disambig) but destroys refusal calibration on ambiguous tasks (BBQ-ambig). The study uses a 28-configuration grid and proposes a new evaluation protocol called Per-Condition Calibration Diagnosis (PCCD). This finding is significant because it reveals that common model compression techniques like knowledge distillation can introduce hidden harms that aggregate bias metrics miss. It affects researchers and practitioners working on instruction-tuned small models, highlighting the need for more nuanced evaluation in AI safety and fairness. The paper reports that for the most biased baseline (SmolLM2-1.7B-Instruct), distillation cuts the context-overriding error rate from 44% to 24% on unambiguous tasks, but on ambiguous tasks, 15% of items where the baseline correctly abstained instead receive stereotype answers. The pattern reproduces on OLMo-2-1B-Instruct, with silence-loss of 8% and filled-silence accounting for 89% of new bias; the two effects are uncorrelated (Spearman ρ=0.19, n.s.).

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 3, 04:00

**Background**: Knowledge distillation is a technique where a smaller 'student' model learns from a larger 'teacher' model, often to reduce computational costs while retaining performance. The BBQ (Bias Benchmark for Question Answering) benchmark evaluates social bias in two regimes: ambiguous contexts, where the correct answer is 'Unknown', and unambiguous contexts, where a definitive answer exists. Refusal calibration refers to a model's ability to appropriately abstain from answering when information is insufficient. The paper argues that aggregate stereotype metrics like CrowS-Pairs and overall BBQ Stereotype Reliance Score conceal per-item harm.

## [LLM-as-a-Judge Blind Spots: The Agentic Formalism Trap](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28641) ⭐️ 8.0/10

This paper introduces the Agentic Formalism Trap and the Evaluative Dissonance Index (D_E), quantifying how LLM-as-a-Judge systems conflate procedural formality with semantic truth under adversarial load. Analyzing 22,500 trajectories across three domains, the authors identify hallucination maneuvers and show the vulnerability is domain-agnostic and architecture-dependent. This research exposes a systemic vulnerability in LLM-as-a-Judge systems, which are widely used for automated evaluation in AI development. The findings could lead to more robust evaluation methods and highlight the need for architecture-specific vigilance filters to ensure reliable AI assessment. The study uses a logistic meta-evaluator to isolate syntactic triggers of evaluator capture (ROC-AUC 0.8779) and demonstrates domain-agnostic transfer via zero-shot Leave-One-Domain-Out (mean ROC-AUC 0.7482). It also shows that distinct simulated swarm topologies induce mathematically disparate semantic blind spots, indicating unstable closed-loop evaluation.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 3, 04:00

**Background**: LLM-as-a-Judge systems use large language models to automatically evaluate outputs of other AI models, often replacing human reviewers for scalability. However, these systems can be vulnerable to adversarial inputs that exploit their reliance on surface-level features. The Agentic Formalism Trap refers to the tendency of such evaluators to mistake procedural correctness for semantic truth, leading to blind spots in evaluation.

The benchmark includes real mobile apps, human-reviewed screens, structured page-relationship annotations, and navigation test specifications. It proposes state-isolated navigation testing to avoid cascading failures and an anchor-referenced list-wise visual evaluation protocol to improve visual-judge reliability. Across six frontier multimodal LLMs, current models can build projects that compile and reach correct pages, but interactive navigation remains unreliable and visual fidelity and maintainability lag.

## [Theoretical Guarantees for LLM Distinguishability via Dynamical Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28667) ⭐️ 8.0/10

This paper provides the first theoretical analysis of classifying large language models (LLMs) by modeling token embeddings as trajectories of a stochastic linear dynamical system. It proves that the misclassification probability decays exponentially with token sequence length, governed by a dynamical discriminability quantity. This work bridges the gap between empirical success and theoretical understanding of a novel LLM classification method, offering guarantees on scalability and transferability. It also opens a new direction of using dynamical systems theory to analyze AI systems, contrasting with the common approach of using AI to model dynamical systems. The paper formalizes classification as a binary hypothesis test between two stochastic linear dynamical systems. It shows that the total variation distance between stationary marginal distributions can be arbitrarily small even when dynamics differ, establishing a fundamental accuracy floor for classifiers ignoring token dynamics. It also introduces an approximate intertwining condition to characterize cross-embedding generalization.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 3, 04:00

**Background**: Dynamical systems theory studies how systems evolve over time, often using state-space models. In the context of LLMs, token embeddings can be viewed as trajectories of a black-box dynamical system, and classification can be performed by comparing prediction residuals of two such systems. This paper provides theoretical guarantees for this approach, addressing questions of scalability and transferability.

The paper introduces OurArk, an architecture where personal AI agents own a versioned, inspectable software body under human custody, enabling governed self-evolution and recursive descent. It is implemented in the open-source Genesis creation engine and Enoch reference agent, with a four-agent, three-descent lineage demonstrated. This addresses a critical gap in personalized AI agents: users lack control over the artifacts that determine future behavior. By enabling human-agent co-development and persistent, controllable agents, OurArk could significantly impact agent design and human-AI collaboration. The software body includes behavior-defining code, prompts, tools, skills, policies, tests, and evolution mechanisms, while memories and credentials remain private instance state. Self-evolution produces isolated candidate changes validated and merged under human control, and descent creates independently versioned descendants with distinct identities and fresh private-state boundaries.

## [Physics-Informed Deep Learning Predicts Steel Fatigue Life from Micrographs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28695) ⭐️ 8.0/10

A new computer vision framework, CV, estimates the fatigue life (log N_f) of lightweight alloy steels directly from optical micrographs, bypassing lengthy mechanical tests. The pipeline combines a seven-stage OpenCV preprocessing routine, a 28-dimensional physics-informed feature extractor, and a CNN regression model trained with a Gaussian negative log-likelihood (GNLL) loss to predict both fatigue life and sample-specific uncertainty. This approach could significantly reduce the time and cost of materials testing, enabling rapid quality control in steel manufacturing. It also demonstrates the potential of physics-informed deep learning in materials science, potentially accelerating the discovery and qualification of new alloys. On a synthetic micrograph benchmark, ResNet-50 achieved R^2 = 0.93, RMSE = 0.18 log-cycles, and macro-F1 = 0.91. The GNLL objective reduced Expected Calibration Error by 76% compared to a mean-squared-error baseline (ECE: 0.089 → 0.021), and Grad-CAM maps confirmed the network attends to metallurgically meaningful features. The pipeline runs in under 65 ms per image and is open-sourced, but validation relies entirely on synthetic micrographs; a domain-transfer study on real field samples is the next step.

**Background**: Fatigue life prediction is critical for structural steels, but traditional mechanical testing is time-consuming, often taking tens to hundreds of hours. ## [WitCert: Sound Runtime Meter for KV-Cache Quantization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28699) ⭐️ 8.0/10

WitCert introduces a provably sound runtime meter for KV-cache quantization, enabling per-request detection of compression damage in deployed LLM serving systems. It provides deterministic and probabilistic bounds on the total variation between exact and compressed attention, with core theorems machine-checked in Lean 4. This addresses a critical gap in LLM serving: current validation relies on offline benchmarks, leaving deployed systems blind to per-request quality degradation. By enabling runtime observability and gating, WitCert can significantly improve the reliability of quantized inference, benefiting both researchers and practitioners in production environments. The meter has two tiers: a deterministic band-norm-witness bound, sound for any cache-preserving black-box quantizer, and a tighter probabilistic certificate for a subtractively-dithered INT8 quantizer under a request-level failure budget. In SGLang, the meter-driven gating restored FP8 quality from 22.8 to 79.7 on hard RULER tasks, and the certified INT8 cache served 1.88 times more KV tokens at the same memory.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 3, 04:00

**Background**: KV-cache quantization reduces memory usage in LLM serving by compressing the key-value cache, but it can introduce errors that degrade output quality. Traditional validation uses offline benchmarks, which do not reflect real-time conditions. WitCert provides a runtime meter that monitors per-request quality, using formal methods like Lean 4 to ensure soundness. This is analogous to DTrace for system observability, but tailored for KV quantization.

WaiT introduces a wavelet-based flow-matching method that decomposes image generation into coarse and fine frequency bands, allowing high-frequency details to remain noise until coarse structures emerge. It achieves a pixel-space FID of 1.43 on ImageNet 512x512 and a state-of-the-art FID of 1.3 with a 2B model, while reducing sampling compute by up to 50%. This work addresses a key limitation of standard flow matching by leveraging the natural frequency hierarchy of images, leading to Pareto-optimal performance and significant compute savings. It sets new state-of-the-art results on ImageNet and demonstrates scalability to high-resolution and video generation, impacting the broader generative modeling community. WaiT uses lossless wavelets to separate generation into coarse and fine bands, with high-frequency bands joining the flow only after coarse structure emerges. It introduces a three-axis evaluation protocol to assess quality at native resolution, addressing the limitations of standard FID, and achieves an FVD of 0.84 on Kinetics-600 for video generation.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 3, 04:00

**Background**: Flow matching is a generative modeling framework that learns to transform a simple distribution into complex data via continuous flows. Wavelet transforms decompose signals into different frequency components, preserving both spatial and frequency information. FID is a common metric for evaluating image generation quality, but it downsampling can discard fine details, motivating the need for more stringent evaluation.

## [Chain Stratification: New Semantics for RDF Rules with Negation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28778) ⭐️ 8.0/10

The paper introduces chain stratification, a novel condition that guarantees unique, lean, and well-defined semantics for RDF rules with default negation, addressing limitations of existing stratification methods. It also provides a prototype implementation to demonstrate practicality. This work solves a fundamental open problem in combining RDF rule languages with default negation, which is crucial for rule-based systems in the Semantic Web. It has significant theoretical and practical implications, potentially enabling more expressive and reliable rule engines for knowledge representation. Chain stratification combines an analysis of potential multistep derivations with integrity constraints to discard impossible cases. Applying rules in any order that respects chain stratification guarantees a unique, lean, and justified RDF graph under negation-as-failure semantics.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 3, 04:00

**Background**: RDF rule languages like N3 or SHACL Rules are used for reasoning over Semantic Web data, but adding default negation is challenging because individual triples lack sufficient information to restrict dependencies, and blank nodes in rule heads can cause order-dependent behavior. Existing stratification methods often fail for RDF rules, motivating the need for a new condition like chain stratification.

## [BSG-VA Reveals Many Passing Tests Don't Validate Bug Fixes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28871) ⭐️ 8.0/10

This paper introduces BSG-VA, a method to analyze validation evidence in LLM repair agents, and finds that 46.0% of positive comparable events carry no bug-discriminating information. A three-arm experiment shows that bug-contrast feedback reduces evidence-inadequate closure by 7.8 percentage points. This work addresses a critical gap in evaluating LLM-based repair agents by systematically measuring the validity of test-passing evidence. The findings have significant implications for the reliability of automated repair systems, as many passing tests may not actually validate the bug fix. The study analyzed 3,730 events across 643 rollouts on 110 tasks, finding that 23.8% of baseline rollouts close with a patch whose entire positive evidence base is non-bug-discriminating. The bug-contrast feedback improved bug-discriminating evidence by 7.4 points (p=0.011) with no detectable cost to repair success, though the effect size was below the prespecified 10-percentage-point threshold.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 3, 04:00

**Background**: LLM repair agents are AI systems that automatically fix software bugs by generating patches and running tests to validate them. However, a passing test may not actually confirm that the bug is fixed, as it might pass for unrelated reasons. BSG-VA (buggy-state/candidate-state/gold-fix validation analysis) captures each validation command at its exact working-tree state, extracts a test-only patch, and replays it on the original buggy code, candidate state, and developer gold fix to classify the evidence.

## [LLMs Avoid Deleting Code, New Study Finds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28887) ⭐️ 8.0/10

A new paper reveals that leading LLMs systematically avoid deleting code during edits, with deletion recall reaching at most 71.7% on SWE-bench Verified tasks. The authors introduce a benchmark called CanItDelete and show that post-training can mitigate this behavior. This issue undermines code maintainability, as test-passing patches often leave dead code behind, and current benchmarks fail to detect it. The findings could reshape how code repair models are evaluated and trained, benefiting software engineering and AI/ML communities. Across five top models, deletion recall is at most 71.7%, and while models locate the right file for over 92% of required deletions, they cut the exact line in under 52% of cases. Additionally, 29.0% of passing patches use a 'Guard-and-Go' pattern, wrapping targeted code instead of removing it.

## [Gated Q-learning: A New Way to Balance Off-Policy Bias and Speed](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28916) ⭐️ 8.0/10

Gated Q-learning introduces a continuous, state-action-dependent gating mechanism that selectively attenuates eligibility traces, smoothly interpolating between Watkins' Q(λ) and Peng's Q(λ). This resolves the long-standing trade-off between off-policy bias and learning speed in Q-learning without relying on importance sampling. This work provides a principled alternative to importance sampling for off-policy multi-step reinforcement learning, potentially improving sample efficiency in complex environments. It offers practitioners a tunable parameter to control the trade-off, which could lead to faster learning in real-world applications. The paper proves that the expected operator remains a contraction mapping and derives its exact fixed point. Empirical evaluations show that intermediate gating enables longer credit-assignment horizons and faster initial learning than either extreme.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 3, 04:00

**Background**: Q-learning is a model-free reinforcement learning algorithm that learns the value of actions. Eligibility traces, such as in Watkins' Q(λ) and Peng's Q(λ), allow multi-step credit assignment but suffer from a trade-off: Watkins' method eliminates off-policy bias but truncates traces, while Peng's method ignores bias for faster learning but injects errors. Importance sampling, a common technique for off-policy correction, fails under Q-learning's greedy target policy because the ratios collapse.

## [FairFund-Bench: New Benchmark Reveals Audit Format Flips LLM Bias Direction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28934) ⭐️ 8.0/10

Researchers introduced FairFund-Bench, a benchmark that systematically varies audit design features (task, comparison context, transparency) to evaluate distributive bias in LLM resource allocation. Testing 14 models on 600 financial aid requests, they found that audit format changes the direction of bias, with models advantaging minorities in individual rating but penalizing some groups in side-by-side ranking. This benchmark addresses the critical issue of inconsistent LLM bias audits, which have previously produced contradictory results for the same models. By revealing that audit format can flip bias direction, it underscores the need for standardized evaluation protocols and has significant implications for fairness in high-stakes AI deployment, such as resource allocation. The benchmark comprises 600 requests generated from human-authored templates calibrated against 1.3M real GoFundMe campaigns, covering three domains, four race and two gender categories, and five causal framings of need. Bias magnitude is several times greater in disguised audits than transparent ones, and causal framing effects exceed demographic effects by an order of magnitude, with models robustly reproducing human deservingness evaluations.

**Background**: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used to allocate scarce resources, raising concerns about biased outcomes based on race and gender. ## [LLM Use and Scientific Productivity: Robust Association Confirmed](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28968) ⭐️ 8.0/10

This paper refutes the claim that the positive association between LLM adoption and scientific productivity is merely a statistical artifact from stopping-time selection. Using multiple robust designs, the authors show that the association persists even when the timing artifact is eliminated. This research settles a critical methodological debate in the emerging field of studying LLM impact on scientific productivity. It provides rigorous evidence that LLM adoption is genuinely linked to productivity gains, which has implications for research evaluation and AI adoption policies. The authors recalibrate RBB's random placebo to the detector's realized flag rate, showing the artifact is too small to explain the productivity changes. They also employ complementary designs: before-and-after comparisons, conservative control groups for difference-in-differences, intensity-based specifications, and rank-based measurements, all yielding positive associations.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 3, 04:00

**Background**: Stopping-time selection bias occurs when the timing of an event (like LLM adoption) is determined by the outcome, potentially creating spurious associations. Event-study designs and difference-in-differences are common quasi-experimental methods used to estimate causal effects from observational data. This paper addresses concerns that such biases might invalidate previous findings on LLM productivity.

## [Point2Radio: Foundation Model for Cross-Scene Radio Field Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28994) ⭐️ 8.0/10

Point2Radio is a foundation model that learns transferable propagation priors from material-aware point clouds, enabling fast prediction of radio fields (e.g., 3D path-gain and power angular spectra) across new scenes in milliseconds on a single GPU. It achieves 0.871 dB mean absolute error on a scene-disjoint split of a 337-scene corpus, reducing error by 76.7% relative to a UNet-style baseline. This work addresses a key limitation in radio field simulation—the need to re-simulate or re-fit per scene—by introducing a model that generalizes across environments. It could significantly accelerate wireless network planning, ray tracing, and 6G research, making high-fidelity radio field prediction accessible for real-time applications. The model uses a common encoder to produce a transmitter-conditioned scene representation from material-aware point clouds, which can be queried at arbitrary receiver locations. Task-specific decoders map this representation to different radio quantities, such as 3D path-gain (PG) fields and power angular spectra (PAS). Light fine-tuning on a target scene further improves adaptation to specific environments.

**Background**: Radio field prediction is essential for wireless communications, but traditional methods like ray tracing are computationally expensive and scene-specific. Foundation models, which are large-scale pre-trained models that can be adapted to various tasks, have shown success in computer vision and NLP, and Point2Radio applies this paradigm to radio propagation. Material-aware point clouds represent a scene's geometry and material properties, which influence how radio waves reflect, diffract, and scatter.

## [Auto-JEPA: Latent World Model for Efficient Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29031) ⭐️ 8.0/10

Auto-JEPA introduces an action-oriented latent world model for autonomous driving that predicts future driving intent via joint-embedding prediction, avoiding dense future reconstruction and requiring no perception annotations. It achieves 91.3 PDMS on NAVSIM v1 and 89.1 EPDMS on NAVSIM v2. This approach could significantly improve the efficiency and generalization of autonomous driving systems by focusing only on planning-relevant features rather than reconstructing the entire future world. It may reduce computational costs and enhance robustness in dynamic environments, benefiting the broader autonomous driving and world model research community. Auto-JEPA keeps the visual encoder frozen, uses no learned trajectory generator, and optimizes only task-specific modules for trajectory representation, intent prediction, and candidate selection. Semantic occlusion experiments show that masking dynamic-agent regions induces an average intent change 2.97x that of equal-area random masking, indicating the model focuses on planning-relevant features.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 3, 04:00

**Background**: World models in autonomous driving typically predict dense future states like videos or occupancy grids, which is computationally expensive and may include irrelevant details. Joint-embedding predictive architectures (JEPA) learn by predicting in representation space rather than reconstructing raw data, which is more efficient and aligns with the idea of focusing on task-relevant information. Auto-JEPA applies this principle to driving by predicting an intent embedding that aligns with the future ego trajectory, then retrieving executable trajectories from a fixed memory.

## [Lookahead Lemma Generation Boosts Neural Network Verifiers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29051) ⭐️ 8.0/10

This paper introduces an inprocessing framework for neural network verification that uses lookahead to derive new lemmas over unstable ReLU phases, which are collected into an implication graph to prune the search space and vivify boolean cuts. The framework is instantiated in two state-of-the-art verifiers, Marabou and α-β-CROWN, and improves their performance, proving up to 34% more instances unsatisfiable. This work addresses a key bottleneck in neural network verification, where branch-and-bound solvers often struggle with large search spaces. By improving the efficiency of two widely used verifiers, it can accelerate the adoption of formal verification in safety-critical applications such as autonomous driving and medical diagnosis. The lookahead procedure derives lemmas over unstable ReLU phases, which are then used to construct an implication graph for pruning and vivification. The framework is generic and can be integrated into other branch-and-bound based verifiers, not just Marabou and α-β-CROWN.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 3, 04:00

**Background**: Neural network verification aims to formally prove properties of neural networks, such as robustness to adversarial perturbations. State-of-the-art verifiers like Marabou and α-β-CROWN use branch-and-bound as their core solving mechanism, which involves splitting the problem into subproblems and deriving bounds. Inprocessing techniques, such as vivification, are commonly used in SAT solving to simplify formulas and improve solver performance.

## [FedSLM: Federated Fine-Tuning of Foundation Models with Heterogeneous Compressed Clients](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29071) ⭐️ 8.0/10

The paper introduces FedSLM, a parameter-centric framework for federated fine-tuning of foundation models with heterogeneous compressed clients. It uses SVD-based decomposition to create self-contained client models with nested manifolds, enabling effective aggregation across clients with varying resource constraints. FedSLM addresses the critical resource-asymmetry challenge in federated learning with foundation models, where institutions with valuable data cannot host billion-parameter models. By enabling heterogeneous clients to participate in fine-tuning while maintaining performance, it could significantly broaden the applicability of federated learning in real-world, resource-constrained settings. FedSLM employs a two-stage protocol: it synchronizes lightweight adapters within compression groups and fuses full-rank reconstructions across groups via structural alignment. It also includes a weak-to-strong elicitation step with an auxiliary confidence loss to transfer aggregated knowledge to the full-scale server, and provides theoretical guarantees for adapter-level aggregation and subspace-alignment bounds.

**Background**: Federated learning enables collaborative model training without centralizing data, but foundation models are often too large for many institutions. Existing approaches like parameter-efficient tuning, pruning, or knowledge distillation each sacrifice some property, such as full-model memory reduction, architectural self-containedness, or representational fidelity. SVD-based decomposition is a common technique to reduce model size while preserving important information, and nested manifolds provide a way to align low-rank subspaces for aggregation.

## [HERO: History-Enriched Rollout Training for Long-Horizon Neural Operators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29135) ⭐️ 8.0/10

HERO introduces a novel training method that augments standard absolute trajectory supervision with relative supervision from the model's own optimization history, using a lagged operator to generate reference rollouts. This approach consistently improves long-horizon accuracy, stable rollout length, and out-of-distribution robustness across nine PDE benchmarks with no inference-time cost. This work addresses the critical problem of error accumulation in autoregressive neural operators for PDEs, which limits their practical use in long-horizon simulations. By leveraging optimization history, HERO offers a new paradigm for stabilizing long-horizon predictions, potentially benefiting scientific computing and ML-based surrogate modeling. HERO ranks candidate rollouts from a lagged operator, the current model, and a perturbed input using metrics like rollout error, spectral discrepancy, energy drift, and error growth, then selects the worst as a reference. This reference is used in a margin-based objective that reweights the ground-truth rollout gradient, and the method is theoretically analyzed.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 3, 04:00

**Background**: Neural operators are machine learning models that learn solution operators for PDEs, enabling fast surrogates for time-dependent problems. In autoregressive rollout, the model recursively applies itself to its own predictions, but errors accumulate over time. Traditional training methods like teacher forcing or rollout training only measure absolute error against ground truth, which may not capture long-horizon failure modes. HERO addresses this by using relative supervision from the model's history.

## [Dataset-Level Membership Inference Attack Exposes Privacy Leakage in Synthetic Face Datasets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29144) ⭐️ 8.0/10

A new study introduces a dataset-level membership inference attack that first identifies the synthetic dataset used to train a face recognizer and then infers the real dataset used to train the generator. Across 11 face recognition models, 11 synthetic datasets, and 7 real datasets, the attack recovers the synthetic training dataset in 100% of cases and identifies the generator's source dataset in 54.5% of cases. This finding is significant because it demonstrates that synthetic face datasets, which are increasingly used to reduce privacy exposure, can still retain dataset-level traces of real training data. This compromises the privacy guarantees of synthetic data and has critical implications for privacy-preserving biometric systems and the deployment of generative models. The attack operates at the dataset level, first identifying the synthetic dataset used to train a face recognizer, then inferring the real dataset used to train the generator. The study evaluates 11 face recognition models, 11 synthetic datasets, and 7 real datasets, achieving 100% accuracy in recovering the synthetic training dataset and 54.5% accuracy in identifying the generator's source dataset.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 3, 04:00

**Background**: Synthetic face datasets are generated by models trained on real face images, and are used to train face recognition systems while aiming to reduce privacy concerns. However, generative models can memorize training data, and this study shows that even at the dataset level, synthetic data can leak information about the real data used to train the generator. Membership inference attacks typically aim to determine whether a specific sample was in a model's training set, but this work extends the concept to the dataset level.

## [Implicit ML Force Fields Speed Up Molecular Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29158) ⭐️ 8.0/10

A new paper introduces implicit machine learning force fields (I-MLFFs), which replace explicit neural network layers with self-consistent fixed-point equations, enabling intermediate representations to be reused across timesteps. This approach achieves 2-5x reductions in compute and memory across three major GNN classes while maintaining accuracy. This advancement significantly improves the efficiency of molecular dynamics simulations, allowing longer trajectories and larger systems within fixed GPU budgets. It could accelerate research in computational chemistry and materials science, enabling new insights into biomolecular and material systems. The method is architecture-agnostic and demonstrated on invariant, equivariant Cartesian tensor, and SO(3)-equivariant spherical-tensor GNNs. It retains full atomistic resolution and the original integration timestep, avoiding spatial or temporal coarse graining.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 3, 04:00

**Background**: Machine learning force fields (MLFFs) use neural networks to predict interatomic forces from atomic configurations, aiming to combine the accuracy of quantum mechanics with the speed of classical potentials. Graph neural networks (GNNs) are a common architecture for MLFFs, but deep networks are computationally expensive. The implicit formulation leverages fixed-point equations, a concept from deep equilibrium models, to achieve deep network accuracy with shallow network efficiency.

## [Memory Provenance Laundering in LLM Agents: A Non-Amplification Firewall](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29167) ⭐️ 8.0/10

This paper identifies a new security vulnerability called memory provenance laundering in LLM agents, where untrusted observations are rewritten as trusted user history during memory consolidation. It introduces PPMF, a lightweight memory middleware that preserves provenance and authorizes tool calls based on action risk. This vulnerability could allow attackers to trigger unauthorized actions in LLM agents by laundering low-trust memory into high-trust context, posing a serious threat to AI safety. The proposed PPMF provides a practical defense, potentially influencing future memory system designs and security standards for AI agents. In schema-grounded evaluations, vulnerable consolidated memories achieved up to 1.000 attack success rate (ASR), while PPMF blocked all unauthorized high-risk actions while allowing confirmed benign actions and low-risk memory use. PPMF is execution-centric and decouples memory usefulness from action authority.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 3, 04:00

**Background**: LLM agents use long-term memory to reuse preferences and workflows, but this memory can be manipulated through prompt injection or untrusted observations. Memory provenance laundering occurs when an external observation is rewritten as user history during consolidation, erasing the low-trust source. Existing defenses like prompt filters and tool guards do not enforce source-authority non-amplification after lossy consolidation, leaving a gap that PPMF addresses.

## [CLIFT: Closed-Loop Fine-Tuning for Humanoid Specialists](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29172) ⭐️ 8.0/10

The paper introduces CLIFT, a non-invasive closed-loop iterative fine-tuning method that adapts closed-weight robot foundation models like Gemini Robotics On-Device (GROD) into humanoid specialists via supervised fine-tuning (SFT) APIs, achieving near-perfect success after two flywheel cycles without accessing model internals. This work addresses a critical limitation in adapting closed-weight robot foundation models, which typically restrict policy improvement to pure imitation learning. By enabling closed-loop improvement through API-compatible data, CLIFT could significantly enhance the deployment of humanoid robots in agile, contact-rich tasks, impacting the robotics and AI/ML fields. CLIFT converts deployment-time reward feedback into supervised data compatible with SFT APIs, enabling closed-loop policy improvement without accessing weights, gradients, likelihoods, or losses. The method was empirically validated on a real humanoid using GROD, showing that direct SFT outperforms a leading open-weight VLA but still falls short of deployment-level mastery, which CLIFT then closes.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 3, 04:00

**Background**: Robot foundation models, such as vision-language-action (VLA) models, are trained on large datasets and show broad competence, but the strongest ones are often closed-source, limiting adaptation. Managed SFT APIs allow users to submit training data and receive a tuned policy without accessing model internals, but they restrict improvement to imitation learning. CLIFT addresses this by using deployment-time feedback to generate supervised data, enabling closed-loop improvement within the API constraint.

## [MoRAE: Flow-Friendly Latents for Text-to-Motion Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29180) ⭐️ 8.0/10

MoRAE introduces a flow-friendly self-supervised latent space for text-to-motion generation, addressing the spectral ill-conditioning and decoder-sensitive alignment issues that cause Motion-JEPA to fail as a frozen encoder. The method achieves state-of-the-art performance with a standard non-autoregressive Flow-Matching DiT. This work provides a geometric diagnosis of why representation autoencoders fail in motion space and offers a practical solution, potentially advancing text-to-motion synthesis and inspiring similar approaches in other domains. It highlights the importance of latent space conditioning for generative models. MoRAE uses a compact bottleneck to distill the JEPA representation, removing weak and redundant directions to stabilize the latent spectrum. Motion-coupled training then aligns the latent geometry with the decoder, reducing the cost of flow errors after decoding.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 3, 04:00

**Background**: Representation Autoencoders (RAEs) have been successful in image generation by using frozen self-supervised encoders to provide semantic features for diffusion or flow models. Motion-JEPA is a self-supervised encoder for motion data, but directly using it as a frozen encoder in text-to-motion generation fails due to spectral ill-conditioning and decoder-sensitive alignment. MoRAE addresses these bottlenecks to create a flow-friendly latent space.

## [SERUM: Multi-Pass Framework for User Behavior Modeling from Egocentric Video](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29181) ⭐️ 8.0/10

SERUM is a multi-pass framework that extracts finite-state behavioral models from unstructured egocentric video using hierarchical VLM annotation and state merging. It alternates between activity-recognition and intent-inference passes, refining labels iteratively to reduce hallucination and temporal conflation. This work addresses a key challenge in building proactive, personalized AI assistants by enabling automatic extraction of interpretable user behavior models from raw screen recordings. It could significantly advance user modeling and human-computer interaction research, providing a scalable pathway for behavioral understanding in real-world settings. The framework was evaluated on 61 egocentric videos across four domains (coding, cooking, physical activities, daily life). It achieves lower perplexity and higher action prediction accuracy than frequency baselines, with the largest gains on structured tasks like coding; human annotators rated final-pass labels as accurate and improved over first-pass labels.

**Background**: Egocentric video, recorded from the user's perspective, is considered an ideal medium for building proactive, personalized AI assistants. SERUM uses hierarchical VLM annotation and state merging to automatically produce interpretable process models without manual annotation.

## [MOSAIC: Fast Secure Outsourcing for Large Transformer Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29221) ⭐️ 8.0/10

MOSAIC introduces a novel matrix-multiplication masking protocol that enables efficient and secure outsourcing of AI computations, achieving orders-of-magnitude faster runtimes for large transformer inference while maintaining security under the decisional LWE and LPN assumptions. This work addresses a critical bottleneck in privacy-preserving machine learning by making secure inference practical for large models like 70B transformers. It could enable confidential AI in data centers with minimal trusted computing base, benefiting both security and ML communities. MOSAIC introduces small noise to multiplication results, relaxing correctness to achieve optimal asymptotic client overhead, and uses random Hadamard rotations to bound error growth across transformer layers. On 70B models, its perplexity is comparable to quantization approaches and matches full-precision BF16 inference on HumanEval.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 3, 04:00

**Background**: Secure outsourcing of AI computations involves a trusted client with limited resources delegating computation to an untrusted server while keeping data and model private. Prior protocols for secure matrix multiplication were too slow for large-scale models. The decisional LWE and LPN assumptions are standard cryptographic hardness assumptions that underpin the security of many schemes, including this one.

## [FBFM: Training-Free Feedback Mechanism Enhances Flow-Matching in World-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29235) ⭐️ 8.0/10

FBFM introduces a training-free asynchronous feedback mechanism that corrects flow-matching predictions at each time step using cross-chunk pairing, improving long-horizon robot control. It is evaluated on DreamZero and LingBot-VA, achieving over 5% success rate improvements on selected LIBERO and RoboTwin2.0 tasks. This mechanism addresses a key limitation of existing world-action models by enabling fine-grained online correction without retraining, which is crucial for reliable long-horizon robot control. It could significantly impact robotics and generative modeling by bridging open-loop flow generation with closed-loop real-world dynamics. FBFM applies a masked pseudoinverse correction to the conditional velocity field during flow matching, using the preceding action chunk and observed image to guide the next chunk's generation. It is training-free and creates an asynchronous loop that corrects errors without waiting for chunk boundaries, improving responsiveness to unexpected events and suppressing drift.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 3, 04:00

**Background**: World-action models (WAMs) predict visual evolution before acting to enhance long-horizon robot control, but they require re-grounding in real observations to maintain reliability. Existing WAMs refresh history or KV cache with ground-truth data between chunks, but this chunk-wise feedback is coarse-grained. Flow matching is a generative modeling framework that learns a time-dependent vector field to transform a simple prior into complex data distributions, and FBFM leverages this framework for fine-grained correction.

## [Selective Prior Calibration Reduces Commonsense-Driven Hallucinations in VLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29240) ⭐️ 8.0/10

The paper introduces Selective Prior Calibration (SPC), a method that selectively suppresses a vision-language model's commonsense prior when it conflicts with visual evidence, improving accuracy on counterfactual images while preserving performance on commonsense images. This addresses a critical reliability issue in vision-language models, where commonsense priors can override clear visual evidence, leading to hallucinations. SPC offers a practical solution that could enhance model trustworthiness in real-world applications, particularly in safety-critical domains. SPC subtracts candidate-level prior-preference estimates from image-conditioned scores with an instance-dependent strength, and only revises the prediction when the resulting score pattern strongly supports an alternative. Experiments show substantial accuracy gains on counterfactual images with minimal impact on commonsense images, and gains generalize across categories and benchmarks.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 3, 04:00

**Background**: Vision-language models (VLMs) often rely on commonsense priors to answer questions, but these priors can cause hallucinations when the visual input depicts atypical states, such as a six-fingered hand. The paper identifies this as commonsense-driven hallucination (CDH) and proposes SPC to calibrate the model's reliance on priors. The method is evaluated on counterfactual and commonsense image benchmarks, demonstrating its effectiveness.

The paper introduces TwT (Translation with Thought), a resource-rational framework for multi-domain machine translation that learns to modulate between intuitive and deliberate reasoning based on difficulty. It is trained via supervised fine-tuning on difficulty-aware chain-of-thought traces and reinforcement learning with a hybrid reward, achieving superior quality with 32-60% less token usage. AgenticRepair addresses this by engineering code-structure, runtime-execution, and commit-history contexts, which are critical for understanding cross-file data flows, crash semantics, and how fragile code patterns were introduced.

Offline evaluations show intent prediction achieves 0.86 F1, drift detection reaches QWK=0.907, and spotlight outperforms baseline by 2.4x with 5x fewer tokens. This addresses the scalability problem of AI-generated code review, shifting focus from low-value style suggestions to correctness, security, and performance. It could significantly improve how human reviewers prioritize their attention in large-scale code changes, especially in AI-assisted development workflows. The system is grounded in a six-theme taxonomy derived from 18,000 code reviews. In an experimental rollout, drift scores reduced code misalignment by 5.76 points (p=0.026), intent prediction received 90.2% approval, and zero defects have been attributed to self-reviewed diffs since launch.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 3, 04:00

**Background**: Traditional code review struggles to keep up with the volume of AI-generated code, and existing AI review tools often over-index on style and best practices. ARCTIC reframes review around understanding developer intent, detecting drift between intent and output, and highlighting critical regions, using techniques like backtranslation and LLM-based ranking.

## [TerraNova: A Foundation Model for the Coupled Human-Earth System](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29527) ⭐️ 8.0/10

TerraNova is a new foundation model that fuses 512 gridded Earth-system fields and 512 national indicators in their native geometries using cross-modal transformers and a hypernetwork. It introduces two contrastive objectives, including a population-weighted alignment between countries and coordinates, to couple the physical and societal data. This work addresses a fundamental geometric challenge in modeling the Anthropocene, potentially enabling more accurate integrated assessments of climate and social systems. It could benefit researchers in climate science, economics, and policy by providing a unified representation that spans both physical and societal dimensions. The model uses dedicated encoders for location, country, time, and task, and a hypernetwork generates a per-query decoder with an evidential head that outputs a predictive distribution. The frozen backbone can reconstruct dense fields from sparse observations and adapt to unseen variables in minutes on consumer hardware.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 3, 04:00

**Background**: Earth-system foundation models typically operate on gridded data that ignore political borders, while societal data are reported for administrative units, creating a geometric mismatch. Cross-modal transformers are a class of models that fuse information from different modalities, such as images and text, into a shared representation. Hypernetworks are neural networks that generate parameters for another network, enabling per-query adaptation.

## [FriendBench: Benchmarking Dyadic Familiarity Inference in Humans and MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29602) ⭐️ 8.0/10

FriendBench is a new benchmark that evaluates whether multimodal large language models (MLLMs) can infer whether two people are familiar or strangers from 20-second interaction clips. The study compares 26 models from seven companies against human panels across text, audio, and video modalities, finding that the best model matches human accuracy but exhibits a bias toward 'stranger'. This benchmark addresses a gap in evaluating social intelligence in MLLMs, which is crucial for applications like human-robot interaction and social AI. The findings highlight that while models can match human accuracy, their decision biases differ, which could impact real-world deployment where balanced judgments are important. The benchmark uses 96 balanced dyads and a consistent prompt type, ensuring that only interaction manner reveals familiarity. The study finds that richer channels (e.g., video) help both humans and models, but only humans benefit from visible behavior on top of speech, and models show a 'stranger' bias due to differences in effective prior rather than discrimination.

## [On-Policy Interaction Relaxes Representational Demands in Imitation Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29617) ⭐️ 8.0/10

The paper introduces OVI, an interactive on-policy imitation learning algorithm that is statistically efficient when the learner can represent the expert's value function, bypassing the need to represent the expert's policy. It also provides a negative result showing that interaction is necessary for efficiency without stronger assumptions. This work provides theoretical clarity on why interactive and value-based methods improve imitation learning, potentially guiding algorithm design in robotics and language model training. It highlights a tradeoff between representational capacity and interaction, which could lead to more efficient learning in practical scenarios. OVI is computationally efficient given access to a linear maximization oracle, and empirically outperforms BC, DAgger, and offline value-based methods, especially when the learner network is less expressive than the expert's. The negative result shows that offline IL must scale with the complexity of the expert policy class without stronger assumptions.

**Background**: Imitation learning (IL) trains agents to mimic expert behavior from demonstrations, but standard methods like Behavior Cloning (BC) suffer from compounding errors when the learner cannot perfectly represent the expert's policy. Two common interventions are interactive querying (on-policy) and value-based estimation, which this paper analyzes theoretically.

## [PLANTOR: LLMs and Symbolic Reasoning for Multi-Robot Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.19135) ⭐️ 8.0/10

PLANTOR is a new framework that combines large language models (LLMs) with Prolog-based symbolic reasoning to generate and execute multi-robot task plans from natural language descriptions. It automatically synthesizes a structured Prolog knowledge base, applies consistency checks, and produces a temporally optimized schedule converted into an executable behavior tree. This work addresses critical challenges in multi-robot task planning, such as explainability and error correction, by keeping the planning and scheduling stages symbolic and inspectable. It demonstrates a hybrid workflow that reduces manual modeling effort while maintaining correctness, which could influence future AI and robotics systems that require reliable and transparent planning. The framework was evaluated on scenarios inspired by the Blocks World and Grippers benchmark across multiple language models, reporting both knowledge-base quality and planning pipeline runtime. It also demonstrated end-to-end execution in a real multi-arm assembly scenario, showing that LLM-generated knowledge bases can reduce manual effort but may still require human correction.

**Background**: Multi-robot task planning involves generating a sequence of actions for multiple robots to achieve a goal, often from high-level task descriptions. Large language models can automate this modeling but may introduce errors, so combining both approaches offers a balance between automation and reliability. Behavior trees are a modular and reactive model for plan execution commonly used in robotics.
